for more details.)
By Claim
B.2
, under the inner product given by (
1.10
),

D^{2}\mathfrak{E}_{0}(\bar{\gamma})

is also self-adjoint Fredholm and has also finite Morse index.
As above, we know that

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathfrak{W}(\bar{\gamma})[\xi,\xi]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\langle\mathrm{Hess}W(t,\bar{\gamma})\xi(t),\xi(t)\rangle_{g}dt,\quad\xi\in W^{1,2}\left(T_{\gamma}\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\tau}(M,\mathbb{I}_{g})\right),

can be given a compact self-adjoint operator on
W^{1,2}\left(T_{\gamma}\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\tau}(M,\mathbb{I}_{g})\right)
. Hence Proposition
B.1
yields:

Proposition 6.4
.

Under Assumption
6.3
, suppose that
m^{0}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
. Then
there exists
0<\delta_{0}<1
such that the following hold:

(i)

If the Hessian of each
W(t,\cdot)
at the critical point
\bar{\gamma}(t)

is positive definite, then

m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{\lambda},\bar{\gamma})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})+m^{0}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

(ii)

If the Hessian of each
W(t,\cdot)
at the critical point
\bar{\gamma}(t)

is negative definite, then

m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{\lambda},\bar{\gamma})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})+m^{0}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

Assumption 6.5
.

Let
(M,g)
be as in Assumption 1.0 in Introduction.
Consider a function
W\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times M,\mathbb{R})
,
and a
C^{2}
Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)
on
\mathbb{R}\times TM
that is
strictly convex with respect to
v
. For some
\tau>0
, we assume that
W
and

\mathbf{L}
are also
\tau
-periodic in time
t
, and that
\mathbf{L}
satisfies

\mathbf{L}(-t,q,-v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t+\tau,q,v)\quad\forall(t,q,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times TM.

Let
\bar{\gamma}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R},M)
satisfy the problem

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&\frac{d}{dt}\big(\partial_{v}\mathbf{L}(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\big)-\partial_{q}\mathbf{L}(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))=0\;\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\\
&\gamma(-t)=\gamma(t)=\gamma(t+\tau)\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.\end{array}\right\}

Assume that for all
t
,
\partial_{q}W(t,\bar{\gamma}(t))=0
,
and that the Hessian of each
W(t,\cdot)
at the critical point
\bar{\gamma}(t)

is either positive definite or negative definite.

As above, let
L(\lambda,t,q,v)=L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)

for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
(t,q,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times TM
.
Then
L
satisfies Assumption
1.20
, and

\bar{\gamma}
solves (
1.35
) with this
L
, therefore is a critical
point of each
C^{2}
functional
{\mathcal{L}}^{E}_{\lambda}:={\mathcal{L}}^{E}_{0}+\lambda{\mathcal{W}}^{E}

on the
C^{4}
Banach manifold
EC^{1}(S_{\tau};M)
, where

{\mathcal{L}}^{E}_{0}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbf{L}(t,\gamma(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\,dt\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{W}}^{E}(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{\tau}W(t,\gamma(t))\,dt.

Let
EW^{1,2}(\bar{\gamma}^{\ast}TM)=\{\xi\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\bar{\gamma}^{\ast}TM)\,|\,\xi(-t)=\xi(t)=\xi(t+\tau)\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\}
with
the inner product given by (
1.10
).
Modifying the arguments in
[
3
, §5.1]
via the technique methods in Appendix
A
,
on
EW^{1,2}(\bar{\gamma}^{\ast}TM)
, we can prove that
D^{2}\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0}(\bar{\gamma})

is self-adjoint Fredholm and has also finite Morse index and that

D^{2}\mathcal{W}^{E}(\bar{\gamma})
is given a compact self-adjoint operator (cf.
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
).
Therefore, by Proposition
B.1
we have:

Proposition 6.6
.

Under Assumption
6.5
, suppose that
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
. Then
there exists
0<\delta_{0}<1
such that the following hold:

(i)

If the Hessian of each
W(t,\cdot)
at the critical point
\bar{\gamma}(t)

is positive definite, then

m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{\lambda},\bar{\gamma})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

(ii)

If the Hessian of each
W(t,\cdot)
at the critical point
\bar{\gamma}(t)

is negative definite, then

m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{\lambda},\bar{\gamma})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

By Proposition
6.2
and
Theorems
1.6
,
1.15
, we arrive at:

Theorem 6.7

(
Alternative bifurcations of Rabinowitz’s type
)
.

Under Assumption
6.1
, suppose that
\mathbf{N}=S_{0}\times S_{1}
,

\bar{\gamma}(0)\neq\bar{\gamma}(\tau)

(
if
\dim S_{0}>0
and
\dim S_{1}>0
)
,
and
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
. Then
one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

The problem (
1.12
)–(
1.13
)
with
L
given by (
6.1
) and
\lambda=0
has a sequence of solutions,
\gamma_{k}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,
which converges to
\bar{\gamma}
in
C^{2}([0,\tau],M)
.

(ii)

There exists
\delta^{\ast}>0
such that
for every non-zero
\lambda\in(-\delta^{\ast},\delta^{\ast})
,
the problem (
1.12
)–(
1.13
)
with
L
given by (
6.1
) and parameter value
\lambda

possesses a solution
\alpha_{\lambda}\neq\bar{\gamma}

such that
\alpha_{\lambda}-\bar{\gamma}

converges to zero in
C^{2}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{N})
as
\lambda\to 0
.

(
Recall that we have assumed
M\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
)

(iii)

For a given neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
\bar{\gamma}
in
C^{1}([0,\tau],M)
,
there is a one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{\mu\}
,
the problem (
1.12
)–(
1.13
)
with
L
given by (
6.1
) and parameter value
\lambda

has at least two distinct solutions in
\mathcal{W}
,
\gamma_{\lambda}^{1}\neq\bar{\gamma}
and
\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
which can also be chosen to satisfy
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{1})\neq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{2})

provided that
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>1
and (
1.12
)–(
1.13
) with parameter value
\lambda

has only finitely many distinct solutions in
\mathcal{W}
.

Similarly, if
\mathbf{N}=\mathrm{Graph}(\mathbb{I}_{g})
and
m^{0}(\mathscr{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
, then
one of the above alternatives (i)–(iii) occurs
with (
1.12
)–(
1.13
), and
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
replaced by
(
1.22
), and
\mathscr{E}_{\lambda}
and
\mathscr{E}_{0}
, respectively.

Proposition
6.4
and Theorem
1.19
directly lead to:

Theorem 6.8

(
Alternative bifurcations of Rabinowitz’s type
)
.

Under Assumption
6.3
, suppose that
m^{0}(\mathfrak{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
.
Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

The problem (
1.29
) with

L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and

\lambda=0
has a sequence of solutions,
\gamma_{k}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,
such that
\gamma_{k}\to\bar{\gamma}
in
\mathcal{X}^{2}_{\tau}(M,\mathbb{I}_{g})

(or equivalently
\gamma_{k}|_{[0,\tau]}-\bar{\gamma}|_{[0,\tau]}
converges to zero in
C^{2}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{N})
).

(ii)

There exists
\delta^{\ast}>0
such that
for every non-zero
\lambda\in(-\delta^{\ast},\delta^{\ast})
,
the problem (
1.29
) with
L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and
parameter value
\lambda
possesses a solution
\alpha_{\lambda}\neq\bar{\gamma}

such that
\|(\alpha_{\lambda}-\bar{\gamma})|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{2}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{N})}\to 0
as
\lambda\to 0
.

(iii)

For a given neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
\bar{\gamma}
in
\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\tau}(M,\mathbb{I}_{g})
,
there exists a one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, the problem (
1.29
) with

L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and parameter value
\lambda

has at least two distinct solutions in
\mathcal{W}
,
\gamma_{\lambda}^{1}\neq\bar{\gamma}
and
\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
which can also be chosen to satisfy
\mathfrak{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{1})\neq\mathfrak{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{2})

provided that
m^{0}_{\tau}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mu},\bar{\gamma})>1
and the problem (
1.29
) with parameter value

\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{0\}
has only finitely many distinct solutions in
\mathcal{W}
.

By Proposition
6.6
and Theorem
1.24
we obtain:

Theorem 6.9

(
Alternative bifurcations of Rabinowitz’s type
)
.

Under Assumption
6.5
, suppose that
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{0},\bar{\gamma})>0
. Then
one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

The problem (
1.35
)
with
L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and
\lambda=0
has a sequence of solutions,
\gamma_{k}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,
which converges to
\bar{\gamma}
in
C^{2}(S_{\tau},M)
.

(ii)

There exists
\delta^{\ast}>0
such that
for every non-zero
\lambda\in(-\delta^{\ast},\delta^{\ast})
,
the problem (
1.35
) with
L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and
parameter value
\lambda
, possesses a solution
\alpha_{\lambda}\neq\bar{\gamma}

such that
\alpha_{\lambda}-\bar{\gamma}

converges to zero in
C^{2}(S_{\tau},{\mathbb{R}}^{N})
as
\lambda\to 0
.

(
Recall that
M\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
)

(iii)

For a given neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
\bar{\gamma}
in
C^{2}(S_{\tau},M)
,
there exists a one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, (
1.35
) with

L_{\lambda}(t,q,v)=\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)+\lambda W(t,q)
and parameter value
\lambda

has at least two distinct solutions in
\mathcal{W}
,
\gamma_{\lambda}^{1}\neq\bar{\gamma}
and
\gamma_{\lambda}^{2}\neq\bar{\gamma}
,
which can also be chosen to satisfy
\mathcal{L}^{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{1})\neq\mathcal{L}^{E}_{\lambda}(\gamma_{\lambda}^{2})

provided that
m^{0}_{\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{E}_{\mu},\bar{\gamma})>1
and (
1.35
) with parameter value
\lambda

has only finitely many solutions in
\mathcal{W}
.

In summary, the above discussion indicates that for a sufficiently smooth Lagrangian function that is strictly convex fiberwise, the corresponding Lagrangian system admits a perturbation of the Lagrangian function near a solution at which the action functional has a non-vanishing nullity, such that a Rabinowitz-type bifurcation occurs near that solution.

Consider Physical Lagrangians (including potential
and electromagnetic forces) given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(t,q,v)=\frac{1}{2}\langle P(t,q)v,v\rangle_{g}+\alpha(t,q)[v]-V(t,q),

(6.2)

where
P(t,q):T_{q}M\to T_{q}M
is self-adjoint and positive definite with respect to

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{g}
,

\alpha
is a
t
-dependent one-form (the magnetic potential),

\alpha(t,q)[v]
denotes the value of
\alpha(t,q)\in T_{q}^{\ast}M
at
v\in T_{q}M
,
and
V
is a
t
-dependent potential energy.
They also satisfy the following respective conditions in the three cases:

(i)

The functions
[0,\tau]\times M\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q)\mapsto V(t,q)
and

\displaystyle[0,\tau]\times TM\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q,v)\mapsto\langle P(t,q)v,v\rangle_{g},\quad[0,\tau]\times TM\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q,v)\mapsto\alpha(t,q)[v]

are
C^{2}
.

(ii)

The functions
\mathbb{R}\times M\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q)\mapsto V(t,q)
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times TM\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q,v)\mapsto\langle P(t,q)v,v\rangle_{g},\quad\mathbb{R}\times TM\to\mathbb{R},\;(t,q,v)\mapsto\alpha(t,q)[v]

are
C^{2}
, and also satisfy for all
(t,q,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times TM
,

P(t,q)=P(t+\tau,\mathbb{I}_{g}(q)),\quad V(t,q)=V(t+\tau,\mathbb{I}_{g}(q)),\quad(\mathbb{I}_{g})^{\ast}\alpha(t+\tau,\cdot)=\alpha(t,\cdot).

(iii)

P
,
\alpha
and
V
satisfy the first three lines in (ii) and
the following equalities

\displaystyle P(-t,q)=P(t,q)=P(t+\tau,q),\quad V(-t,q)=V(t,q)=V(t+\tau,q),

\displaystyle-\alpha(-t,q)=\alpha(t,q)=\alpha(t+\tau,q)\quad\text{for all $(t,q)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times M$.}

Then Theorems
6.7
,
6.8
,
6.9

are applicable to
\mathbf{L}
in (
6.2
)
provided that conditions (i), (ii) and (iii)
are satisfied, respectively,

When
M
is
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we can construct more general examples than those in Theorems
6.7
,
6.8
,
6.9
.

For a real
\tau>0
, consider a
C^{3}
function

L:(-1,1)\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.
Suppose that
L(\lambda,t,q,v)
is strictly convex in
v
for
all
(\lambda,t,q)
. Let
R
be a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
which is either the product
V_{0}\times V_{1}
of
two linear subspaces
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

or the graph
\mathrm{Graph}(E)
of an orthogonal matrix
E
of order
n
,
i.e.,
R=\{(x^{\top},(Ex)^{\top})^{\top}\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
.
For each
\lambda\in(-1,1)
, let
x_{\lambda}\in C^{3}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy
the following boundary value problem:

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{d}{dt}\big(\partial_{v}L_{\lambda}(t,x(t),\dot{x}(t))\big)-\partial_{q}L_{\lambda}(t,x(t),\dot{x}(t))=0\;\forall t\in[0,\tau],\\
x\in C^{2}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n}),\;(x(0)^{\top},x(\tau)^{\top})^{\top}\in R\quad\hbox{and}\\
\partial_{v}L_{\lambda}(0,x(0),\dot{x}(0))[v_{0}]=\partial_{v}L_{\lambda}(\tau,x(\tau),\dot{x}(\tau))[v_{1}]\quad\forall(v_{0}^{\top},v_{1}^{\top})^{\top}\in R.\end{array}\right\}

(6.3)

That is,
x_{\lambda}
is a critical point of the
C^{2}
functional

\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}:C^{1}_{R}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathbb{R},\;\gamma\mapsto\int^{\tau}_{0}L_{\lambda}(t,x(t),\dot{x}(t))dt.

(6.4)

It is also assumed that
(-1,1)\times[0,\tau]\ni(\lambda,t)\mapsto x_{\lambda}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is
C^{1}
. Let
\mathbf{H}_{R}={\bf H}_{V_{0}\times V_{1}}
if
R=V_{0}\times V_{1}
,
and
\mathbf{H}_{R}={\bf H}_{E}=W^{1,2}_{E}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
if
R=\text{Graph}(E)
.
By Proposition
3.9
,
the Gâteaux derivative
B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\in{\mathscr{L}}_{s}({\bf H}_{R})
at
x_{\lambda}
of the gradient
\nabla{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
is a self-adjoint Fredholm operator given by

\displaystyle(B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\xi,\eta)_{1,2}=\int_{0}^{\tau}\Bigl(\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\bigl(P_{\lambda}(t)\dot{\xi}(t),\dot{\eta}(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\bigl(Q_{\lambda}(t)\xi(t),\dot{\eta}(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle+\bigl(\dot{\xi}(t),Q_{\lambda}(t)\eta(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\bigl(R_{\lambda}(t)\xi(t),\eta(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Bigr)\,dt

for any
\xi,\eta\in{\bf H}_{R}
, where

{P}_{\lambda}(t)=\partial_{vv}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,x_{\lambda}(t),\dot{x}_{\lambda}(t)\bigr)
,
{Q}_{\lambda}(t)=D_{{q}{v}}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,x_{\lambda}(t),\dot{x}_{\lambda}(t)\bigr)
,
{R}_{\lambda}(t)=D_{{q}{q}}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,x_{\lambda}(t),\dot{x}_{\lambda}(t)\bigr)
.
Since
L
is
C^{3}
, it is easily verified that
B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})
is differentiable in
\lambda
and

\displaystyle(\frac{d}{d\lambda}B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\xi,\eta)_{1,2}=\int_{0}^{\tau}\Bigl(\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\bigl(\frac{d}{d\lambda}P_{\lambda}(t)\dot{\xi}(t),\dot{\eta}(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\bigl(\frac{d}{d\lambda}Q_{\lambda}(t)\xi(t),\dot{\eta}(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

(6.5)

\displaystyle+\bigl(\dot{\xi}(t),\frac{d}{d\lambda}Q_{\lambda}(t)\eta(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\bigl(\frac{d}{d\lambda}R_{\lambda}(t)\xi(t),\eta(t)\bigr)_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Bigr)\,dt.

Now suppose
{\rm ker}B_{0}(x_{0})\neq 0
. Then
{\rm ker}B_{0}(x_{0})
consists of solutions
\zeta
of
the following linear boundary value problem:

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{d}{dt}\big(P_{0}(t)\dot{\zeta}(t)+Q_{0}(t)\zeta(t)\big)-\big(Q_{0}(t)^{\top}\dot{\zeta}(t)+R_{0}(t)\zeta(t)\big)=0\;\forall t\in[0,\tau],\\
\zeta\in C^{2}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n}),\;(\zeta(0)^{\top},\zeta(\tau)^{\top})^{\top}\in R\quad\hbox{and}\\
(P_{0}(0)\dot{\zeta}(0)+Q_{0}(0)\zeta(0),v_{0})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=(P_{0}(\tau)\dot{\zeta}(\tau)+Q_{0}(\tau)\zeta(\tau),v_{1})_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\quad\forall(v_{0}^{\top},v_{1}^{\top})\in R.\end{array}\right\}

(6.6)

By Proposition
B.1
, we immediately obtain:

Proposition 6.10
.

Under the above assumptions, suppose that
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})>0
. Then there exists
0<\delta_{0}<1
such that the following hold:

(i)

If the self-adjoint operator
\left.\frac{d}{d\lambda}B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\right|_{\lambda=0}
defined by (
6.5
) is positive definite on the solution space of (
6.6
), then

m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})+m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

(ii)

If the operator
\left.\frac{d}{d\lambda}B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\right|_{\lambda=0}

is negative definite on the solution space of (
6.6
), then

m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},x_{\lambda})=\begin{cases}m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})+m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0}),&0<\lambda<\delta_{0},\\[4.0pt]
m^{-}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0}),&-\delta_{0}<\lambda<0.\end{cases}

Similarly, if
R=\text{Graph}(E)
and
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})>0
, then
(i) and (ii) hold.

This, together with the Theorems
3.7
,
4.4
,
leads to:

Theorem 6.11

(
Alternative bifurcations of Rabinowitz’s type
)
.

Suppose that
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})>0
, and that one of the conditions (i) and (ii) in Proposition
6.10
holds. Then one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

The problem (
6.3
) with
\lambda=0
has a sequence of nonzero solutions
\{x_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
in
C^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{n})
that converges to the
trivial solution (i.e.,
x_{k}\to 0
in
C^{2}
).

(ii)

For every
\lambda
sufficiently close to
0
but
\lambda\neq 0
, there exists a nonzero solution
y_{\lambda}
of (
6.3
) with parameter
\lambda
, such that
y_{\lambda}\to 0
in
C^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
\lambda\to 0
.

(iii)

Given any neighborhood
\mathfrak{W}
of
0
in
C^{1}_{R}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there exists a one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
0
(i.e.,
\Lambda^{0}\subset(0,\delta)
or
\Lambda^{0}\subset(-\delta,0)
for some
\delta>0
) such that for every
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, problem (
6.3
) with parameter
\lambda
has at least two distinct nonzero solutions
y_{\lambda}^{1},y_{\lambda}^{2}\in\mathfrak{W}
. Moreover, if
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},0)>1
and problem (
6.3
) with parameter
\lambda
has only finitely many solutions in
\mathfrak{W}
, then these two solutions can be chosen to satisfy
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(y^{1}_{\lambda})\neq\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(y^{2}_{\lambda})
.

Similarly, if
R=\text{Graph}(E)
and
m^{0}(\mathcal{E}_{0},x_{0})>0
, then
one of the above alternatives (i)–(iii) occurs.

The results analogous to Theorems
6.8
and
6.9
can be readily formulated.
We omit them. Instead, we consider a concrete example.

6.2
Example: planar simple pendulum

Consider the planar simple pendulum equation:

\ddot{u}+\frac{g}{l}\sin u=0,

(6.7)

where
g
is the universal gravitational constant,
l
is the length of the pendulum, and one end of the pendulum is fixed at the origin. It is well known that there are two kinds of nontrivial
motions of this planar simple pendulum: rotations around the origin and oscillations.
The linearized equation of (
6.7
) at the zero solution is

l\ddot{u}(t)+gu(t)=0
,
which has the general solution
u=C_{1}\cos(\omega t)+C_{2}\sin(\omega t)
, where
\omega=\sqrt{\frac{g}{l}}
. These solutions have period
T:=\frac{2\pi}{\omega}
.

T
-periodic solutions of (
6.7
) are critical points of the functional

\Phi(u)=\int^{T}_{0}\textsf{L}(t,u(t),\dot{u}(t))dt

(6.8)

on
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
, where

\textsf{L}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{2}ly^{2}+g\cos x
.
From the above arguments, we see that
0\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
is
a critical point of
\Phi
with nullity
m^{0}(\Phi,0)=2
.
Let
W(\lambda,t,x)
be a
C^{3}
function on
(-1,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is
T
-periodic in time
t
.
We also assume

W_{x}(\lambda,t,0)\equiv 0,\quad W_{xx\lambda}(0,\cdot,0)\neq 0\quad\text{and either $W_{xx\lambda}(0,\cdot,0)\geq 0$ or
$W_{xx\lambda}(0,\cdot,0)\leq 0$}.

(6.9)

A clear example of the function
W
satisfying the above conditions is

W(\lambda,t,x)=(1+\sin(\frac{2\pi}{T}t))\sin\lambda\cos x.

(6.10)

Define
L(\lambda,t,x,y):=\textsf{L}(t,x,y)+W(\lambda,t,x)
, and let
\Phi_{\lambda}
denote the corresponding action functional defined on
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
.
The first equality in (
6.9
) implies that

0\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})

is a critical point of each
\Phi_{\lambda}
.
Let
B_{\lambda}(0)
be the Gâteaux derivative of the gradient
\nabla{\Phi}_{\lambda}

at
0\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
. Then

\mathrm{ker}B_{0}(0)=\{u=C_{1}\cos(\omega t)+C_{2}\sin(\omega t)\,|\,C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
Note that

\displaystyle{P}_{\lambda}(t)=\partial_{yy}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,0,0\bigr)=l,\qquad{Q}_{\lambda}(t)=D_{{x}{y}}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,0,0\bigr)=0,

\displaystyle{R}_{\lambda}(t)=D_{{x}{x}}{L}_{{\lambda}}\bigl(t,0,0\bigr)=-g+W_{xx}(\lambda,t,0).

By (
6.5
), we obtain the following identity for all
\xi,\eta\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
,

(\frac{d}{d\lambda}B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\big|_{\lambda=0}\xi,\eta)_{1,2}=\int_{0}^{T}W_{xx\lambda}(0,t,0)\xi(t)\eta(t)\,dt.

(6.11)

It follows from the assumptions in (
6.9
) that

\frac{d}{d\lambda}B_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\big|_{\lambda=0}
is either positive definite
or negative definite.
Since
m^{0}(\Phi,0)=2>0
.
By apply Theorem
6.11
(with
E=I_{n}
being the identity matrix of order
n
)
to this
L
, we readily deduce that one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i)

The forced simple pendulum

m\ell\ddot{u}(t)+g\sin u(t)=W_{x}(0,t,u(t))

(6.12)

has a sequence of nontrivial
T
-periodic solutions
\{u_{k}\}^{\infty}_{k=1}

such that
u_{k}|_{[0,T]}
converges to zero in
C^{2}([0,T])
.

(ii)

There exists
\delta^{\ast}>0
such that
for every
\lambda\in(-\delta^{\ast},\delta^{\ast})\setminus\{0\}
,
the forced simple pendulum

m\ell\ddot{u}(t)+g\sin u(t)=W_{x}(\lambda,t,u(t))

(6.13)

possesses a nontrivial
T
-periodic solution
u_{\lambda}

such that
u_{\lambda}|_{[0,T]}
converges to zero in
C^{2}([0,T])
)
as
\lambda\to 0
.

(iii)

For a given neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of zero in

C^{2}([0,T])
, there exists a one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{0\}
, the problem (
6.13
)
has at least two distinct nontrivial solutions
u_{\lambda}^{1}
and
u_{\lambda}^{2}

whose restrictions to
[0,T]
belong to
\mathcal{W}
.
Moreover, since
m^{0}(\Phi,0)=2>1
, these two solutions can also be chosen to satisfy

\int^{T}_{0}\textsf{L}(t,u_{\lambda}^{1}(t),\dot{u}_{\lambda}^{1}(t))dt+\int^{T}_{0}W(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}^{1}(t))dt\neq\int^{T}_{0}\textsf{L}(t,u_{\lambda}^{2}(t),\dot{\gamma}_{\lambda}^{2}(t))dt+\int^{T}_{0}W(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}^{2}(t))dt

provided that the problem (
6.13
) has only finitely many distinct solutions,
and their restrictions to
[0,T]
belong to
\mathcal{W}
.

Remark 6.12
.

When
W(\lambda,t,x)
has a form
\lambda\mathbf{W}(t,x)
, where

\mathbf{W}(t,x)
is a
C^{3}
function on
(-1,1)\times\mathbb{R}
that is
T
-periodic in time
t

and satisfies the following conditions:

\mathbf{W}_{x}(t,0)\equiv 0,\quad\mathbf{W}_{xx\lambda}(\cdot,0)\neq 0\quad\text{and either $\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\geq 0$ or
$\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\leq 0$}.

(6.14)

We have a
C^{2}
functional
\mathfrak{W}
on
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
defined by

\mathfrak{W}(u)=\int^{T}_{0}\mathbf{W}(t,u(t))dt

with critical point
0\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}/(T\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R})
.
When
\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\geq 0
(resp.

\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\leq 0
),
D^{2}\mathfrak{W}(0)
is positive definite (resp. negative definite)
on
\mathrm{ker}B_{0}(0)=\{u=C_{1}\cos(\omega t)+C_{2}\sin(\omega t)\,|\,C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
By applying
[
24
, Theorem 6.6]
to
\alpha=\Phi
and
\beta=\mathfrak{W}
, we obtain:

Conclusion
.
For each sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, eauation

m\ell\ddot{u}(t)+g\sin u(t)=\lambda W_{x}(t,u(t))

(6.15)

has at least two solutions
(\lambda_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})
such that

\mathfrak{W}(u_{\epsilon})=\epsilon
(resp.
\mathfrak{W}(u_{\epsilon})=-\epsilon
)
provided that
\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\geq 0
(resp.

\mathbf{W}_{xx}(\cdot,0)\leq 0
). Moreover,
\lambda_{\epsilon}\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Therefore, our above example cannot be derived from
[
24
, Theorem 6.6]
.

Appendix A
Proofs of some lemmas

Exponential map

Let
M
be a
n
-dimensional,
C^{k}
-smooth manifold.
Its tangent bundle
TM
is a
C^{k-1}
-smooth manifold of dimension
2n
,
whose points are denoted by
(x,v)
, with
x\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
.
Let
g
be a
C^{k-1}
Riemannian metric on
M
.
Let
\varphi:U\to\varphi(U)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},\;q\mapsto\varphi(q)=(x^{1}(q),\cdots,x^{n}(q))

be a coordinate chart on
M
,
g_{ij}=g(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})

and
(g^{ij})=(g_{ij})^{-1}
. Then the Christoffel symbols

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}g^{kj}\Big(\frac{\partial g_{li}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{\partial g_{lj}}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{l}}\Big)

are
C^{k-2}
and hence the exponential map
\exp:TM\to M
is a
C^{k-2}
map.
There exists a fibrewise convex neighborhood
\mathcal{U}(0_{TM})
of the zero section of
TM
such that the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}:\mathcal{U}(0_{TM})\to M\times M,\;(q,v)\mapsto(q,\exp_{q}v)

(A.1)

is a
C^{k-2}
immersion.

For a
W^{1,2}
-curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
, a
W^{1,2}
vector field
V
along
\gamma
and
t\in\gamma^{-1}(U)
we can write

V(t)=\sum_{j}v^{j}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}|_{\gamma(t)}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j}\dot{\gamma}^{j}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}|_{\gamma(t)},

where
\gamma^{j}(t)=x^{j}(\gamma(t))
. The (
W^{1,2}
) covariant derivative of
V
along
\gamma

\frac{DV}{dt}=\sum_{i}\frac{Dv^{i}}{dt}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{\gamma(t)}=\sum_{i}\Big(\frac{dv^{i}}{dt}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}(\gamma(t))\dot{\gamma}^{j}(t)v^{k}(t)\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{\gamma(t)}.

(A.2)

The vector field
V
is called parallel if
\frac{DV}{dt}\equiv 0
.

Let
\pi:TM\to M
be the bundle projection and
\mathscr{U}=\pi^{-1}(U)
.

\varphi
induces a chart on
M
,

\Phi:\mathscr{U}\to\varphi(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\;(q,v)\mapsto(x^{1}(q,v),\cdots,x^{n}(q,v),u^{1}(q,v),\cdots,u^{n}(q,v)),

(A.3)

where
x^{i}(q,v)=x^{i}(q)
and
u^{i}(q,v)=dx^{i}(q)[v]
for
i=1,\cdots,n
, i.e.,

v=\sum^{n}_{i=1}u^{1}(q,v)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}|_{q}
.
It is computed in the standard textbooks in Riemannian geometry that

\displaystyle d\mathbb{F}(q,0)\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(q,0)\Big]=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(q),\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(q)\Big),\quad d\mathbb{F}(q,0)\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}(q,0)\Big]=\Big(0,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(q)\Big).

(A.4)

Claim A.1
.

Let
k>2
. For an even and
\tau
-periodic
C^{k-2}
-curve
\gamma:{\mathbb{R}}\to M
,
there exist unit orthogonal parallel
C^{k-2}
frame fields along
\gamma
,

\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
, such that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle(e_{1}(-t),\cdots,e_{n}(-t))=(e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t)),

(A.5)

\displaystyle(e_{1}(t+\tau),\cdots,e_{n}(t+\tau))=(e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t)).

(A.6)

Proof.

Starting with a unit orthogonal frame at
T_{\gamma(0)}M
and using the parallel
transport along
\overline{\gamma}
with respect to the Levi-Civita
connection of the Riemannian metric
g
we get a unit orthogonal
parallel
C^{5}
frame field
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\gamma}^{\ast}TM,\;t\mapsto(e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t))
.

Firstly, we prove that (
A.5
) is satisfied.
In fact, let
(U;x^{j})
be a local coordinate system around a point in
{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}})
.
Then we can write

e_{k}(t)=\sum^{n}_{i=1}e^{l}_{k}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}({\gamma}(t))\quad\forall t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U),\quad k=1,\cdots,n.

Since
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
is a parallel frame field,

\frac{d}{dt}e^{i}_{l}(t)+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(t))\dot{{\gamma}}^{j}(t)e^{k}_{l}(t)=0,\quad\forall t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U),\quad i=1,\cdots,n.

Note that
{\gamma}(-t)={\gamma}(t)
implies
t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U)
if and only if
-t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U)
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(e^{i}_{l}(-t))=-\dot{e}^{i}_{l}(-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(-t))\dot{{\gamma}}^{j}(-t)e^{k}_{l}(-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(-t))\frac{d}{dt}({\gamma}^{j}(-t))e^{k}_{l}(-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(t))\frac{d}{dt}({\gamma}^{j}(t))e^{k}_{l}(-t).

It follows that

\{e_{1}(-\cdot),\cdots,e_{n}(-\cdot)\}
is also a parallel frame field along
{\gamma}
.
Since
e_{k}(t)
and
e_{k}(-t)
agree at
t=0
,
k=1,\cdots,n
,
by the theorem of existence and uniqueness of ODE we obtain (
A.5
).

Next, we prove that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there holds

\displaystyle(e_{1}(k\tau-t),\cdots,e_{n}(k\tau-t))=(e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t))\quad\forall t\in[0,k\tau].

(A.7)

Since for any
t\in[0,k\tau]
it holds that
t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U)
if and only if
-t\in{\gamma}^{-1}(U)
, as above we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(e^{i}_{l}(k\tau-t))=-\dot{e}^{i}_{l}(k\tau-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(k\tau-t))\dot{{\gamma}}^{j}(k\tau-t)e^{k}_{l}(k\tau-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(-t))\frac{d}{dt}({\gamma}^{j}(k\tau-t))e^{k}_{l}(k\tau-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{i}_{jk}({\gamma}(t))\frac{d}{dt}({\gamma}^{j}(t))e^{k}_{l}(k\tau-t),\quad l=1,\cdots,n.

Therefore
[0,k\tau]\to e_{l}(t)
and
[0,k\tau]\to e_{l}(k\tau-t)

are parallel frame fields along
\gamma|_{[0,k\tau]}
and have the same value at
t=k\tau/2
.
It follows that
e_{l}(k\tau-t)=e_{l}(t)
for any
t\in[0,k\tau]
and
l=1,\cdots,n
.

Finally, we prove (
A.6
). For any
t>0
, let us choose
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
t<k\tau
. Then

e_{l}(\tau+t)=e_{l}((k+1)\tau-(\tau+t))=e_{l}(k\tau-t)=e_{l}(t).

If
t<-\tau
, this and (
A.5
) lead to
e_{l}(\tau+t)=e_{l}(-\tau-t)=e_{l}(\tau+(-\tau-t))=e_{l}(-t)=e_{l}(t)
.
If
-\tau\leq t<0
then
0\leq t+\tau<\tau
. By (
A.7
) and (
A.5
) we derive

e_{l}(\tau+t)=e_{l}(\tau-(\tau+t))=e_{l}(-t)=e_{l}(t)
.
Summarizing these (
A.6
) is proved.

Note
: Since
\gamma
is even, as a loop
\gamma:S_{\tau}\to M
is contractible. Thus
\gamma^{\ast}TM\to S_{\tau}
has an orthogonal
trivialization. This can only lead to the existence of an unit orthogonal frame fields along
\gamma

satisfying (
A.5
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

By Lemma
2.6
it suffices to prove the third and fourth assertions.
Follow the notations above Lemma
3.1
.

Step 1
(
Prove that
(\lambda,t)\mapsto{\bf u}_{\lambda}(t)
is continuous, and
C^{2}
with respect to
t
).
By (
\spadesuit
) in the first paragraph in Section
3.1.1
and (
3.2
), for all
t\in[0,\tau]
we have

\mathbb{F}\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)\Big)=\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\exp_{\overline{\gamma}(t)}\Big(\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)\bigg)\Big)=\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\gamma_{\lambda}(t)\Big).

(A.8)

By (
3.4
),
(\lambda,t)\mapsto\gamma_{\lambda}(t)
is a continuous map from
\Lambda\times[0,\tau]
into
the open subset
{\bf U}_{3\iota}(\gamma_{\mu}([0,\tau]))
of
M
. These and (
\clubsuit
) in the first paragraph in Section
3.1.1

imply the composition

\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\to TM,\;(\lambda,t)\mapsto\Big(\mathbb{F}|_{\mathcal{W}(0_{TM})}\Big)^{-1}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\gamma_{\lambda}(t))=\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)\Big)

is continuous, and
C^{2}
with respect to
t
. Since
g
is a
C^{6}
Riemannian metric on
M
, we obtain

\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\to\mathbb{R},\;(\lambda,t)\mapsto g\Big(\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)\Big),\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),e_{j}(t)\Big)\Big)={\bf u}_{\lambda}^{j}(t)

is continuous, and
C^{2}
with respect to
t
, for each
j=1,\cdots,n
.

Step 2
(
Prove that
(\lambda,t)\mapsto\dot{\bf u}_{\lambda}(t)
is continuous
).
Fix a point
t_{0}\in[0,\tau]
. Let
\varphi:U\to\varphi(U)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

be a coordinate chart centered at
\overline{\gamma}(t_{0})

with
\varphi(q)=(x^{1}(q),\cdots,x^{n}(q))
. For example, we can take
U=\phi_{\overline{\gamma}}(t_{0},B^{n}_{2\iota}(0))

and
\varphi=(\phi_{\overline{\gamma}}(t_{0},\cdot))^{-1}
.
It has the induced chart
(\pi^{-1}(U),\Phi)
on
TM
as in (
A.3
).
Let
J=(\overline{\gamma})^{-1}(U)
, which is an open neighborhood of
t_{0}
in
[0,\tau]
.
For each
t\in J
, we have two bases of
T_{\overline{\gamma}(t)}M
,

e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}|_{\overline{\gamma}(t)},\cdots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}|_{\overline{\gamma}(t)}.

Hence there exists a unique non-degenerate matrix
(A_{ij}(t))
of order
n
such that

e_{i}(t)=\sum^{n}_{j=1}A_{ij}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}|_{\overline{\gamma}(t)},\quad i=1,\cdots,n.

It follows that

\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)=\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)\sum^{n}_{j=1}A_{ij}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}|_{\overline{\gamma}(t)}=\sum^{n}_{j=1}\Big(\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{ij}(t)\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}|_{\overline{\gamma}(t)}.

Let
\varphi(\overline{\gamma}(t))=(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t))
.
Then for each
t\in J
we have

\displaystyle\Phi\Big(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)e_{i}(t)\Big)=\Big(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{i1}(t),\cdots,\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{in}(t)\Big).

It is clear that
\varphi\times\varphi:U\times U\to B^{n}_{2\iota}(0)\times B^{n}_{2\iota}(0)
given by
\varphi\times\varphi(x,y)=(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))

is a chart on
M\times M
centered at
(\overline{\gamma}(t_{0}),\overline{\gamma}(t_{0}))
.
Take a small neighborhood
J_{0}\subset J
of
t_{0}
in
[0,\tau]
such that

d_{g}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\overline{\gamma}(t_{0}))<\iota
for all
t\in J_{0}
. Then (
3.3
) implies

\displaystyle d_{g}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t),\overline{\gamma}(t_{0}))<2\iota,\quad\forall(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times J_{0}.

(A.9)

Hence
\{\gamma_{\lambda}(t)\,|\,(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times J_{0}\}
is contained in the chart
(U,\varphi)
. Let

(\varphi\times\varphi)\left(\overline{\gamma}(t),\gamma_{\lambda}(t)\right)=\left(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t))\right).

By this, (
A.8
) and (
A
) we obtain that for all
(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times J_{0}
,

\displaystyle\Phi\circ(\mathbb{F})^{-1}\circ(\varphi\times\varphi)^{-1}\left(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t))\right)

\displaystyle=\Big(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{i1}(t),\cdots,\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{in}(t)\Big).

(A.10)

Note that
\Psi:=\Phi\circ\left(\mathbb{F}|_{\mathcal{W}(0_{TM})}\right)^{-1}\circ(\varphi\times\varphi)^{-1}
is a
C^{5}
diffeomorphism onto its image set.
Taking the derivative of
t
for the equation in (
A
) we arrive at

\displaystyle D\Psi\Big(x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t)),\cdots,x^{n}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t))\Big)\Big[\frac{d}{dt}x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\frac{d}{dt}x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t)),

\displaystyle\hskip 312.9803pt\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}x^{n}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t)\Big]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{d}{dt}x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)\frac{d}{dt}A_{i1}(t),\cdots,\sum^{n}_{i=1}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)\frac{d}{dt}A_{in}(t)\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{d}{dt}x^{1}(\overline{\gamma}(t)),\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}x^{n}(\overline{\gamma}(t),\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{i1}(t),\cdots,\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{in}(t)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\forall(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times J_{0}.

(A.11)

Since all
A_{ij}
are
C^{5}
,
D\Psi
is
C^{4}
,
(\lambda,t)\mapsto{\bf u}_{\lambda}(t)
is continuous (by Step 1), and

(\lambda,t)\mapsto x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t))\quad\hbox{and}\quad(\lambda,t)\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}x^{1}(\gamma_{\lambda}(t))

are continuous in
\Lambda\times J_{0}
(by Assumption
1.2
),
it follows from (
A
) that

\Lambda\times J_{0}\ni(\lambda,t)\mapsto\Big(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{i1}(t),\cdots,\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{i}(t)A_{in}(t)\Big)=\Big(\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{1}(t),\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{n}(t)\Big)(A_{ij}(t))

is continuous. Moreover, all
(A_{ij}(t))
are invertible. These lead to

\Lambda\times J_{0}\ni(\lambda,t)\mapsto\Big(\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{1}(t),\cdots,\frac{d}{dt}{\bf u}_{\lambda}^{n}(t)\Big)

is continuous. Since
t_{0}\in[0,\tau]
is arbitrarily chosen, the required claim is proved.
∎

Appendix B
Morse indexes of a class of abstract functionals

Let
H
be a separable real Hilbert space with inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
, and let
\mathcal{F}^{sa}(H)

denote the set of all bounded linear self-adjoint Fredholm operators on
H
.
For a
C^{0}
path
t\mapsto B_{t}\in\mathcal{F}^{sa}(H)
,
t\in(-\eta,\eta)
,
we have a family of functionals on

H
,

f_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2}(B_{t}x,x),\quad x\in H,

for which the origin
0\in H
is a critical point with finite Morse index.
Suppose that
\dim{\rm ker}B_{0}=N>0
and
t\mapsto B_{t}
is differentiable at
0
.
The derivative of
B_{t}
at
t=0
, denoted by
\dot{B}_{0}
, belongs to
\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
.
Consider the symmetric bilinear form
Q
on

\operatorname{ker}B_{0}
given by

Q(x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(x,B_{t}(y))\big|_{t=0}=(x,\dot{B}_{0}(y)),\quad x,y\in\operatorname{ker}B_{0}.

The following result is contained in the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 3.23]
.
For the sake of completeness, we present a detailed proof.

Proposition B.1
.

Under the above assumptions, suppose that
Q
is non-degenerate and has the Morse index
m^{-}(Q)=q
.
Then there exists
0<\delta_{0}<\eta
such that

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t},0)=\begin{cases}m^{-}(Q)+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad 0<t<\delta_{0},\\
m^{0}(f_{0},0)-m^{-}(Q)+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad-\delta_{0}<t<0.\end{cases}

(B.1)

In particular, we have the following special cases:

m^{-}(f_{t},0)=\begin{cases}m^{-}(f_{0},0),&0<t<\delta_{0},\\
m^{-}(f_{0},0)+m^{0}(f_{0},0),&-\delta_{0}<t<0,\end{cases}\quad\text{if }Q\text{ is positive definite,}

and

m^{-}(f_{t},0)=\begin{cases}m^{-}(f_{0},0)+m^{0}(f_{0},0),&0<t<\delta_{0},\\
m^{-}(f_{0},0),&-\delta_{0}<t<0,\end{cases}\quad\text{if }Q\text{ is negative definite.}

Proof.

Step 1
.
Consider the complexification
H_{\mathbb{C}}=\{z=x+iy\,|\,x,y\in H\}
of the Hilbert space
H
,
equipped with the complex linear structure and complex inner product defined by

\displaystyle(a+ib)(x+iy)=(ax-by)+i(bx+ay),\quad a,b\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=(x_{1},x_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}+(y_{1},y_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}+i[(y_{1},x_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}-(x_{1},y_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}]

for
z_{1}=x_{1}+iy_{1},\,z_{2}=x_{2}+iy_{2}
.
The complexified operator
\tilde{B}_{t}
of
B_{t}
is the complex linear extension of
B_{t}
defined
for
z=x+iy\in H_{\mathbb{C}}
by

\tilde{B}_{t}(z)=B_{t}x+iB_{t}y.

It is a self-adjoint operator with respect to the complex inner
product
\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The spectrum of
\tilde{B}_{t}
is the same as that of
B_{t}
(which is a subset of the real numbers), and for a real spectral point
\lambda
, the complex eigenspace is the complexification of the real eigenspace.
Since
B_{0}
is a bounded linear self-adjoint Fredholm operator,

0\in\sigma(B_{0})
is an isolated point in
\sigma(B_{0})
.
Therefore, we can choose
c>0
such that
\pm c\notin\sigma(B_{0})
.
From the assumption of the continuous family
\{B_{t}\}
there exists

\delta\in(0,\eta]
such that

\pm c\notin\sigma(B_{t})
for all
|t|\leq\delta
. Let
\Gamma_{c}=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|\zeta|=c\}
. Define
the complex projection operators
\tilde{P}_{t}
on
H_{\mathbb{C}}
by

\tilde{P}_{t}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\Gamma_{c}}(\zeta I_{H_{\mathbb{C}}}-\tilde{B}_{t})^{-1}\,d\zeta,\quad|t|\leq\delta,

which are orthogonal projections (self-adjoint and idempotent with respect to the complex inner product).
All these
\tilde{P}_{t}
have the constant rank equal to
N=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\text{ker}\tilde{B}_{0}
, and

\tilde{E}_{t}:=\tilde{P}_{t}(H_{\mathbb{C}})=\sum_{|\zeta|<c}\text{ker}(\tilde{B}_{t}-\zeta I_{H_{\mathbb{C}}})=E_{t}\oplus iE_{t},

where
E_{t}:=\sum_{-c<\zeta<c}\text{ker}({B}_{t}-\zeta I_{H})
.
Since
\tilde{E}_{t}
is the complexification of a real subspace
E_{t}
, and hence when
restricted to
H\subset H_{\mathbb{C}}
, each
\tilde{P}_{t}
yields the real orthogonal projection

{P}_{t}
from the real space
H
onto
E_{t}
.
Moreover, it is not hard to prove that
the path
t\mapsto\tilde{P}_{t}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H_{\mathbb{C}})
,
t\in(-\delta,\delta)
, is
continuous and differentiable at
0
, with derivative given by

\dot{\tilde{P}}_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{c}}(\zeta I_{H_{\mathbb{C}}}-\tilde{B}_{0})^{-1}\dot{\tilde{B}}_{0}(\zeta I_{H_{\mathbb{C}}}-\tilde{B}_{0})^{-1}d\zeta.

Step 2
.
Consider the orthogonal complementary
E_{t}^{\bot}
of
E_{t}
in
H
.
Since
B_{t}
is self-adjoint, the eigenspaces corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of
B_{t}

are orthogonal. Hence

\sum_{\zeta<-c}\text{ker}({B}_{t}-\zeta I_{H})\oplus\sum_{\zeta>c}\text{ker}({B}_{t}-\zeta I_{H})\subset E_{t}^{\bot}.

Let
F_{t}:=\sum_{\zeta<-c}\text{ker}({B}_{t}-\zeta I_{H})
. Then
we have

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{0},0)

\displaystyle=\dim F_{0},

(B.2)

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t},0)

\displaystyle=m^{-}(f_{t}|_{E_{t}},0)+m^{-}(f_{t}|_{F_{t}},0)

\displaystyle=m^{-}(f_{t}|_{E_{t}},0)+\dim F_{t}.

(B.3)

Since
\sigma(B_{0})\cap(-\infty,-c)
consists of finitely many eigenvalues with finite (algebraic) multiplicity, say
\lambda_{0,1},\cdots,\lambda_{0,k}
, where each
\lambda_{0,i}
has multiplicity
m_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,k
, by the arguments at the end of
[
31
, §135]
we can shrink
\delta>0
so that for each
t\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
\sigma(B_{t})\cap(-\infty,-c)
consists exactly of
k
eigenvalues with finite (algebraic) multiplicity,
\lambda_{t,1},\cdots,\lambda_{t,k}
, and each
\lambda_{t,i}

is near
\lambda_{0,i}
and also has multiplicity
m_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,k
. Hence

\displaystyle\dim F_{t}=\dim F_{0},\quad t\in(-\delta,\delta).

(B.4)

Step 3
. Define
p=N-q
. Let
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{N}
be all eigenvalues of
Q
, and let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}
be the unit eigenvectors corresponding to them, which form a unit
orthogonal basis of
E_{0}
. Then
P_{t}e_{1},\ldots,P_{t}e_{N}
form a unit
orthogonal basis of
E_{t}
since all
P_{t}
are orthogonal projections. Hence

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t}|_{E_{t}},0)=\text{dimension of the negative definite space of matrix
$\big(\left(B_{t}\circ P_{t}(e_{i}),P_{t}(e_{j})\right)\big)$.}

(B.5)

We can assume that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{q}<0
and
0<\mu_{q+1}\leq\ldots\leq\mu_{N}
.
Define
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\min_{j}|\mu_{j}|
.

Following the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 3.23]
, for
x,y\in{\rm ker}B_{0}
there holds:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{t}\cdot B_{t}\circ P_{t}(x),P_{t}(y)\right)-\left(\dot{B}_{0}(x),y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{1}{t}\cdot(B_{t}-B_{0})-\dot{B}_{0}\right)\circ P_{t}(x),P_{t}(y)\right)+\left(\frac{1}{t}\cdot(P_{t}(x)-x),B_{0}(P_{t}(y))\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\dot{B}_{0}(P_{t}(x)-x),P_{t}(y)\right)+\left(\dot{B}_{0}(x),P_{t}(y)-y\right).

Let
\dot{P}_{0}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H)
denote the derivative of the path
P_{t}
at
t=0
.
Since, when
t\to 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|{B_{0}(P_{t}(e_{i}))}\|\to\|{B_{0}(P_{0}(e_{i}))}\|=\|{B_{0}e_{i}}\|=0,

\displaystyle\|(P_{t}(e_{i})-e_{i})\|\to\|P_{0}e_{i}-e_{i}\|=0,

\displaystyle\|{\frac{1}{t}\cdot(P_{t}(e_{i})-e_{i})}\|\to\|\dot{P}_{0}e_{i}\|,

there exists
0<\delta_{0}\leq\delta
such that for all
|t|<\delta_{0}
and
i,j=1,\cdots,N
,

\left|\left(\frac{1}{t}\cdot(P_{t}(e_{i})-e_{i}),B_{0}(P_{t}(e_{j}))\right)+\left(\dot{B}_{0}(P_{t}(e_{i})-e_{i}),P_{t}(e_{j})\right)+\left(\dot{B}_{0}(e_{i}),P_{t}(e_{j})-e_{j}\right)\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{N},

and hence

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{1}{t}\cdot B_{t}\circ P_{t}(e_{i}),P_{t}(e_{j})\right)-\left(\dot{B}_{0}(e_{i}),e_{j}\right)\right|<\frac{\epsilon}{N}.

Let
\lambda_{1}(t)\leq\lambda_{2}(t)\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{N}(t)
be
the eigenvalues of matrix

\big(\left(B_{t}\circ P_{t}(e_{i}),P_{t}(e_{j})\right)\big)
.
By Weyl’s Perturbation Theorem for eigenvalues of Hermitian matrices
(cf. Problem 4.3.P1 on
[
11
, page 256]
or the first exercise on
[
11
, page 408]
),
we obtain

|\frac{1}{t}\lambda_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}|<\epsilon\quad\text{and hence}\quad|\frac{1}{t}\lambda_{i}(t)|\geq\epsilon

for all
0<|t|<\delta_{0}
and
i=1,\cdots,N
.
Then for
0<t<\delta_{0}
there holds:

\lambda_{i}(t)\begin{cases}<-\epsilon t,&i=1,\cdots,q,\\
>\epsilon t,&i=q+1,\cdots,m;\end{cases}

and for
-\delta_{0}<t<0
there holds:

\lambda_{i}(t)\begin{cases}>-\epsilon t,&i=1,\cdots,q,\\
<\epsilon t,&i=q+1,\cdots,m.\end{cases}

It follows from these and (
B.5
) that

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t}|_{E_{t}},0)=\begin{cases}q&\;\text{for}\quad 0<t<\delta_{0},\\
p&\;\text{for}\quad-\delta_{0}<t<0.\end{cases}

(B.6)

This, together with (
B.2
)-(
B.4
), leads to

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t},0)=m^{-}(f_{t}|_{E_{t}},0)+\dim F_{0}=\begin{cases}q+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad 0<t<\delta_{0},\\
p+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad-\delta_{0}<t<0.\end{cases}

(
B.1
) is proved.
∎

Let
B\in\mathcal{F}^{sa}(H)
, and let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be
an alternative inner product on
H
that induces a norm equivalent to the one induced by
(\cdot,\cdot)
.
Since
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is bounded and coercive with respect to the original norm,
the Lax–Milgram theorem implies that there exists a bounded linear invertible operator

\Xi:H\to H
such that for all
u,v\in H
,

\displaystyle\langle u,v\rangle=(\Xi u,v)\quad\text{and hence}\quad\langle\Xi^{-1}u,v\rangle=(u,v).

(B.7)

Claim B.2
.

The operator
\Xi^{-1}B
is also Fredholm, and self-adjoint with respect to
the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

Proof.

The first claim is clear since
\Xi
(and
consequently
\Xi^{-1}
) is bounded and invertible.
To prove the second one, let
(\Xi^{-1}B)^{\ast}
denote the adjoint operator
of
\Xi^{-1}B
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Then
for any
u,v\in H
,

\displaystyle\langle(\Xi^{-1}B)^{\ast}u,v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u,\Xi^{-1}Bv\rangle=\langle\Xi^{-1}Bv,u\rangle=(Bv,u)=(Bu,v)=\langle\Xi^{-1}Bu,v\rangle,

where the third and the final equalities come from (
B.7
).
Hence
(\Xi^{-1}B)^{\ast}=\Xi^{-1}B
, i.e.,

\Xi^{-1}B
is self-adjoint with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
∎

As a consequence of Claim
B.2
and Proposition
B.1
, we have:

Corollary B.3
.

Under the above assumptions, suppose that
Q
is non-degenerate and has the Morse index
m^{-}(Q)=q
.
Then there exists
0<\delta_{0}<\eta
such that

\displaystyle m^{-}(f_{t},0)=\begin{cases}m^{-}(Q)+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad 0<t<\delta_{0},\\
m^{0}(f_{0},0)-m^{-}(Q)+m^{-}(f_{0},0)&\;\text{for}\quad-\delta_{0}<t<0.\end{cases}
